Visual witness description of PDF page 8 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal line segment representing a crossing path with marked points from left to right: vertex a, vertex a', vertex v, a vertical dashed division line, vertex g, and endpoint w. Above the segment, a horizontal span arrow from a to v is labeled "head sum α"; a vertical downward arrow points directly to vertex a', labeled "selected suffix α'"; a horizontal span arrow from g to w is labeled "tail sum β". Below the segment, the left section from a to v is labeled "H", and the right section from g to w is labeled "F \ H".
```

These splits involve distinct original segments, so all can be made simultaneously. Their pieces and the remaining heads are still disjoint. Thus $\sum_i\alpha_i^2$ together with all the large positive cross terms is at most $\rho(f)^2\le1$. The small cross terms cost at most $8s^2$, and the pure tail terms cost at most $4s^2$. We have controlled both corrected tests. It remains to use them together so that their common correction disappears.

Test $x+y$ against $(f+sv^+)/\sqrt{1+12s^2}$ and $x-y$ against $(f+sv^-)/\sqrt{1+12s^2}$. The mixed support terms vanish, and the two evaluations involving $c$ cancel in the average. Letting the norming errors tend to zero proves <a href="#eq:paired-parameter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-parameter">[eq:paired-parameter]</a>. Finite tails are dense in $Q_HX$, so <a href="#eq:paired-parameter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-parameter">[eq:paired-parameter]</a> holds for every $y=Q_Hy$. If $\|x\|>0$, maximize its right side by taking $s=\|y\|/(12\|x\|)$; zero values follow by a limit. The maximum is the right side of <a href="#eq:paired" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired">[eq:paired]</a>. Replacing $y$ by $ty$ in <a href="#eq:paired-parameter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-parameter">[eq:paired-parameter]</a> proves <a href="#eq:paired-two-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-two-parameters">[eq:paired-two-parameters]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-paired-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-paired</summary>

*Proof of the midpoint assertion in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* For a unit $x\in X$, choose $H$ with $\|x-P_Hx\|<\eta$. For every unit $y\in\ker P_H$, Lemma <a href="#lem:paired" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:paired">6</a> applied to $P_Hx$ and $ty$ gives $$\frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}
 \ge\sqrt{(1-\eta)^2+t^2/12}-\eta
 \qquad(0<\eta<1).$$ Take the infimum over these $y$, the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces, and let $\eta\downarrow0$. This proves the asserted bound, since it is independent of the unit center $x$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:general-forest"></a>
#### The coordinate completion on a general forest

The paired argument used finite support and ancestral cuts, rather than finite component heights. We record its resulting scope, while keeping the distinction between a coordinate completion and a full dual explicit. Let $T$ be any countably infinite rooted forest in which every vertex has finitely many ancestors. Define $\rho_T$ by <a href="#eq:segment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-norm">[eq:segment-norm]</a> with $T$ in place of $\mathcal F$, let $J(T)$ be the completion, and put $$X(T)=\overline{\operatorname{span}}^{\|\cdot\|}\{e_v^*:v\in T\}
 \subset J(T)^*.$$ Thus $X(T)$ is the completion of finite coordinate vectors in the polar norm. We do not identify it with all of $J(T)^*$.

A finite ancestral set cuts each segment into a prefix and suffix. The projections $P_H,Q_H$ are therefore contractive on $J(T)$ and $X(T)$, and finite vectors outside $H$ are dense in $Q_HX(T)$. Every finite set has finite ancestral closure, so an increasing finite ancestral exhaustion gives $P_Hx\to x$ for all $x\in X(T)$. The proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:paired" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:paired">6</a> applies unchanged to these objects: there are finitely many nonzero masses $m_g$, their initial segments are disjoint, and unattached roots give no crossing term. These are all the properties used in the test construction, the simultaneous suffix cuts, and the passage to completed tails. Consequently <a href="#eq:paired" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired">[eq:paired]</a> and <a href="#eq:paired-two-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-two-parameters">[eq:paired-two-parameters]</a> hold in $X(T)$, and the same arbitrary-center approximation gives <a id="eq:general-forest-modulus"></a>$$\label{eq:general-forest-modulus}
 \widehat\delta_{X(T)}(t)\ge\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1\qquad(t>0).$$ This also explains why reflexivity is used for the example’s structural identification, rather than as a hypothesis of the paired estimate.

For $T=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, the coordinate completion $X(T)$ is the countably branching James-tree predual $B_\infty$, as described in Banakh (Banakh 2000, sec. 2.II) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Banakh2000](#ref-Banakh2000)] following Ghoussoub–Maurey–Schachermayer. We give its path obstruction in the displayed norm. Each root-path functional $p_v=\sum_{w\preceq v}e_w^*$ again has norm one. The antichain calculation in <a href="#eq:antichain-isometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:antichain-isometry">[eq:antichain-isometry]</a> uses only disjoint segments and applies to $X(T)$ as well. Thus, for any $\phi\in X(T)^*$ and finitely many children of a fixed vertex, $$\left|\sum_j a_j\phi(e_{v^\frown j}^*)\right|
 \le\|\phi\|\left(\sum_j a_j^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Taking $a_j=\phi(e_{v^\frown j}^*)$ shows that these evaluations are square summable, and hence tend to zero. The child increments are therefore weakly null against the whole continuous dual. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:bounded-tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bounded-tree">5</a> with $m=M=c=1$ proves $\overline\delta_N(\alpha/(2\beta))=0$ for every equivalent norm $\alpha\|\cdot\|\le N\le\beta\|\cdot\|$ on this coordinate completion. This argument does not require it to be reflexive.

<a id="sec:diamonds"></a>
### From approximate midpoints to diamonds

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### An infinite family of approximate midpoints

Support restrictions disappear when infinitely many separated perturbations are available. This is the only compactness step in the metric proof. Approximate-midpoint lenses are central to AMUC (Dilworth et al. 2016, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]; the broader midpoint method is attributed to Enflo’s unpublished argument in (F. Baudier et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. The configuration below is a separated midpoint family, called a spider in (Basset, Lancien, and Procházka 2026, Definition 3.7; Definition 6.15 and Proposition 6.16) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[BassetLancienProchazka2026](#ref-BassetLancienProchazka2026)]. We derive its precise squared deficit from Lemma <a href="#lem:paired" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:paired">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-lens"></a>
<div id="lem:lens" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lens"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *Let $R>0$, $\varepsilon>0$, $x\in X$, and $(y_j)_{j\ge1}\subset X$ satisfy $$\|x\pm y_j\|\le R,\qquad\|y_i-y_j\|\ge\varepsilon>0
 \quad(i\ne j).$$ Then $$\label{eq:lens}
 R^2-\|x\|^2\ge\varepsilon^2/48.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lens</summary>

*Proof.* Convexity gives $\|x\|,\|y_j\|\le R$. Given $\eta>0$, choose finite ancestral $H$ with $\|Q_Hx\|<\eta$. The bounded sequence $(P_Hy_j)$ is in a finite-dimensional space, so some distinct $i,j$ satisfy $\|P_H(y_i-y_j)\|<2\eta$. Put $z=(y_i-y_j)/2$. Since $$x+z=\tfrac12((x+y_i)+(x-y_j)),\quad
 x-z=\tfrac12((x-y_i)+(x+y_j)),$$ we have $\|x\pm z\|\le R$. Also $$\|P_Hz\|<\eta,\quad\|Q_Hz\|\ge\varepsilon/2-\eta,
 \quad\|P_Hx\|\ge\|x\|-\eta.$$ Both $P_Hx\pm Q_Hz$ have norm at most $R+2\eta$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:paired" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:paired">6</a>, $$(R+2\eta)^2\ge\|P_Hx\|^2+\|Q_Hz\|^2/12.$$ For $0<\eta<\varepsilon/2$, let $\eta\downarrow0$ to obtain <a href="#eq:lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens">[eq:lens]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Adding the loss over the diamond levels

Figure <a href="#fig:diamond" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:diamond">3</a> shows the replacement whose loss we now estimate.

<figure id="fig:diamond">

<figcaption>One diamond replacement, with only three of the countably many paths shown. All new edges have length one. The old endpoints are at distance two, as are any two distinct new middle vertices.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-diamond"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:diamond`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \draw[thick] (0,0)--(2,0);
 \fill (0,0) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};
 \fill (2,0) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};
 \draw[-{Stealth},thick] (2.8,0)--(4,0);
 \coordinate (u) at (5,0); \coordinate (v) at (9,0);
 \foreach \h/\lab in {1.4/1,0.45/2,-1.4/j}{
   \draw[thick] (u)--(7,\h)--(v);
   \fill (7,\h) circle (2pt) node[above] {$w_{\lab}$};
 }
 \node at (7,-0.5) {$\vdots$};
 \fill (u) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};
 \fill (v) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};
 \node at (1,-1.9) {one old edge};
 \node at (7,-1.9) {countably many two-edge paths};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
On the left, a single horizontal edge connecting two endpoints labeled u and v, labeled below as "one old edge". A horizontal arrow points to the right diagram, which depicts vertices u and v connected by multiple two-edge paths bowed outward, labeled below as "countably many two-edge paths". Paths pass through intermediate middle vertices: top path through w_1, second path through w_2, vertical ellipsis dots (· · ·), a lower path through w_j, and vertical ellipsis dots (· · ·) below w_j.
```


Each parent edge will consume the same positive amount of squared stretch. Earlier quantitative diamond arguments accumulate convexity losses across levels (Lee and Naor 2004, Lemma 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[LeeNaor2004](#ref-LeeNaor2004)], or use a self-improvement induction (Johnson and Schechtman 2009, Lemma 1 and Proposition 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[JohnsonSchechtman2009](#ref-JohnsonSchechtman2009)]. The countably branching AMUC form appears in (F. Baudier et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BaudierEtAl2017](#ref-BaudierEtAl2017)]. Here Lemma <a href="#lem:lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lens">7</a> supplies a fixed squared loss, which we can add directly.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lens-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lens</summary>

*Proof of the distortion assertion in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $f:D_k\to X$ satisfy $s d_k\le\|f(u)-f(v)\|\le Ds d_k$. Divide by $s$, so $f$ is noncontracting and $D$-Lipschitz. Distances between old vertices double at each refinement: a new path between old vertices consists of two-edge passages which either return to their starting vertex or contract to an old edge, and every old path can be lifted with twice its length. Therefore the endpoints of an edge of $D_m$ have distance $2^{k-m}$ in $D_k$. Distinct middle vertices replacing that edge also have distance $2^{k-m}$ in $D_k$.

Put $$L_m=\sup_{\{u,v\}\in E(D_m)}
       \frac{\|f(v)-f(u)\|}{2^{k-m}},\qquad 0\le m\le k.$$ These suprema need not be attained; all that is used is $1\le L_m\le D$. Fix one parent edge at level $m<k$, put $\ell=2^{k-m-1}$, and let $w_j$ be its middle vertices. Set $$x=\frac{f(v)-f(u)}{2\ell},\qquad
 y_j=\frac{f(w_j)-(f(u)+f(v))/2}{\ell}.$$ The child edges give $\|x\pm y_j\|\le L_{m+1}$, and the middle-vertex distances give $\|y_i-y_j\|\ge2$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lens">7</a> and then taking the supremum over the parent edges yields $$L_m^2\le L_{m+1}^2-1/12.$$ Summing over $m$ gives $$D^2\ge L_k^2\ge L_0^2+k/12\ge1+k/12.$$ For $k=0$ this is just $D\ge1$. This completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

To verify the renorming assertion in the introduction, suppose $a\|z\|\le N(z)\le b\|z\|$. An embedding with distortion at most $D$ for $N$ has distortion at most $bD/a$ for $\|\cdot\|$. Thus uniform embeddings for an equivalent norm would contradict the distortion bound just proved.

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A finite ancestor-closed head and some of its gates. Black large nodes belong to $H$; circled nodes are gates. Shading indicates two complementary descendant cones. Only finitely many of the countably many branches are drawn. In particular an untouched component begins with a gate at its root.",
      "figure_id": "fig:forest-gates",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:forest-gates",
          "exact_quote": "A finite ancestor-closed head and some of its gates.  Black\nlarge nodes belong to $H$; circled nodes are gates.  Shading indicates\ntwo complementary descendant cones.  Only finitely many of the\ncountably many branches are drawn.  In particu",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.1cm,y=1cm,\n head/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=2.3pt},\n gate/.style={circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=2.8pt},\n vertex/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=1.4pt},font=\\small]\n\\fill[black!5] (1.4,1.1)--(.6,-.25)--(2.2,-.25)--cycle;\n\\fill[black!5] (5,2.3)--(3.3,-.3)--(6.7,-.3)--cycle;\n\\draw (-.05,2.2)--(-1,1.05)--(-1.65,0);\n\\draw (-1,1.05)--(-.35,0);\n\\draw (-.05,2.2)--(1.4,1.05);\n\\draw (1.4,1.05)--(.85,0);\n\\draw (1.4,1.05)--(1.95,0);\n\\node[head,label=above:$r_2$] at (-.05,2.2) {};\n\\node[head,label=left:$s$] at (-1,1.05) {};\n\\node[gate,label=below:$g_1$] at (-1.65,0) {};\n\\node[gate,label=below:$g_2$] at (-.35,0) {};\n\\node[gate,label=right:$g_3$] at (1.4,1.05) {};\n\\node[vertex] at (.85,0) {};\n\\node[vertex] at (1.95,0) {};\n\\node at (-1,.0) {$\\cdots$};\n\\node at (.65,1.4) {$\\cdots$};\n\\node at (1.4,-.25) {$\\cdots$};\n\\node at (-.35,2.85) {$H=\\{r_2,s\\}$};\n\\draw (5,2.2)--(4.1,1.05)--(3.65,0);\n\\draw (4.1,1.05)--(4.55,0);\n\\draw (5,2.2)--(5.9,1.05)--(5.45,0);\n\\draw (5.9,1.05)--(6.35,0);\n\\node[gate,label=above:{$g_4=r_3$}] at (5,2.2) {};\n\\node[vertex] at (4.1,1.05) {};\n\\node[vertex] at (5.9,1.05) {};\n\\node at (5,1.15) {$\\cdots$};\n\\node at (4.1,-.2) {$\\vdots$};\n\\node at (5.9,-.2) {$\\vdots$};\n\\node at (5,2.85) {An untouched component};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two rooted tree structures. Left tree: root node labeled r_2 (large solid black circle) with text label \"H = {r_2, s}\" above it. An edge descends left to node s (large solid black circle) and an edge descends right to node g_3 (circled white node). From s, edges descend to circled white nodes g_1 and g_2, with horizontal dots · · · indicating further branches. Below g_1 and g_2 are shaded downward-pointing cones with dotted bases (· · ·). From g_3, two edges descend to vertical downward arrows, with dotted base (· · ·) and vertical dots. Right tree: labeled above as \"An untouched component\", having root node g_4 = r_3 (circled white node). From g_4, edges descend to two intermediate uncircled nodes (with horizontal dots · · · between them), each leading to a shaded downward-pointing cone with a dotted base (· · ·)."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The threshold cut for a crossing segment. Its head has $f$-sum $α$ and its selected suffix has $f$-sum $α′$. When $αβ > 0$ and $|α| > 2sm_{g}$, the sign choice gives $αα′",
      "figure_id": "fig:paired-cut",
      "pdf_page": 8,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:paired-cut",
          "exact_quote": "The threshold cut for a crossing segment. Its head has\n$f$-sum $\\alpha$ and its selected suffix has $f$-sum $\\alpha'$.\nWhen $\\alpha\\beta>0$ and $|\\alpha|>2sm_g$, the sign choice gives\n$\\alpha\\alpha'<0$ and $|\\alpha'|\\ge2sm_g$.\nCutting immed",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small]\n \\fill[blue!5] (-.4,-.6) rectangle (6.25,1.25);\n \\fill[orange!8] (6.25,-.6) rectangle (9.6,1.25);\n \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(9.2,0);\n \\draw[dashed,gray] (6.25,-.7)--(6.25,.8);\n \\foreach \\x in {0,1,2,3,4,5,6,6.6,7.5,8.3,9.2}\n   \\fill (\\x,0) circle (1.7pt);\n \\node[below] at (0,0) {$a$};\n \\node[below] at (4,0) {$a'$};\n \\node[below] at (6,0) {$v$};\n \\node[below] at (6.6,0) {$g$};\n \\node[below] at (9.2,0) {$w$};\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (0,.35)--(6,.35)\n   node[midway,above=5pt] {head sum $\\alpha$};\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (4,.95)--(6,.95)\n   node[midway,above=5pt] {selected suffix $\\alpha'$};\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (6.6,.35)--(9.2,.35)\n   node[midway,above=5pt] {tail sum $\\beta$};\n \\node at (2,-.5) {$H$};\n \\node at (8,-.5) {$\\mathcal F\\setminus H$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal line segment representing a crossing path with marked points from left to right: vertex a, vertex a', vertex v, a vertical dashed division line, vertex g, and endpoint w. Above the segment, a horizontal span arrow from a to v is labeled \"head sum α\"; a vertical downward arrow points directly to vertex a', labeled \"selected suffix α'\"; a horizontal span arrow from g to w is labeled \"tail sum β\". Below the segment, the left section from a to v is labeled \"H\", and the right section from g to w is labeled \"F \\ H\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "One diamond replacement, with only three of the countably many paths shown. All new edges have length one. The old endpoints are at distance two, as are any two distinct new middle vertices.",
      "figure_id": "fig:diamond",
      "pdf_page": 10,
      "source_evidence": [

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(2,0);\n \\fill (0,0) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};\n \\fill (2,0) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};\n \\draw[-{Stealth},thick] (2.8,0)--(4,0);\n \\coordinate (u) at (5,0); \\coordinate (v) at (9,0);\n \\foreach \\h/\\lab in {1.4/1,0.45/2,-1.4/j}{\n   \\draw[thick] (u)--(7,\\h)--(v);\n   \\fill (7,\\h) circle (2pt) node[above] {$w_{\\lab}$};\n }\n \\node at (7,-0.5) {$\\vdots$};\n \\fill (u) circle (2pt) node[left] {$u$};\n \\fill (v) circle (2pt) node[right] {$v$};\n \\node at (1,-1.9) {one old edge};\n \\node at (7,-1.9) {countably many two-edge paths};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "On the left, a single horizontal edge connecting two endpoints labeled u and v, labeled below as \"one old edge\". Paths pass through intermediate middle vertices: top path through w_1, second path through w_2, vertical ellipsis dots (· · ·), a lower path through w_j, and vertical ellipsis dots (· · ·) below w_j."
```
